					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">Edward Heath</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 70 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-70" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<span class="vector-dropdown-label-text">70 languages</span>
	</label>
	<div class="vector-dropdown-content">

<p><b>Sir Edward Richard George Heath</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:small; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Garter">KG</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_British_Empire" title="Order of the British Empire">MBE</a></span> (9 July 1916&#160;&#8211;&#32;17 July 2005), commonly known as <b>Ted Heath</b>, was a British politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> from 1970 to 1974 and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Leader of the Conservative Party (UK)">Leader of the Conservative Party</a> from 1965 to 1975. Heath also served for 51 years as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">Member of Parliament</a> from 1950 to 2001. Outside politics, Heath was a <a href="/wiki/Yachting" title="Yachting">yachtsman</a>, a musician, and an author.
</p><p>Born to a lady's maid and a carpenter, Heath was educated at a grammar school in Ramsgate, Kent (<a href="/wiki/Chatham_House_Grammar_School" title="Chatham House Grammar School">Chatham House Grammar School</a> for boys) and became a leader within student politics while studying at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a>. He served as an officer in the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Artillery" title="Royal Artillery">Royal Artillery</a> during the <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">Second World War</a>. He worked briefly in the <a href="/wiki/Civil_Service_(United_Kingdom)" title="Civil Service (United Kingdom)">Civil Service</a>, but resigned in order to stand for Parliament, and was elected for <a href="/wiki/Bexley_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Bexley (UK Parliament constituency)">Bexley</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1950_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1950 United Kingdom general election">1950 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He was promoted to become <a href="/wiki/Chief_Whip_of_the_Conservative_Party" title="Chief Whip of the Conservative Party">Chief Whip</a> by <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden" title="Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a> in 1955, and in 1959 was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Cabinet of the United Kingdom">Cabinet</a> by <a href="/wiki/Harold_Macmillan" title="Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a> as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Labour_(United_Kingdom)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Labour (United Kingdom)">Minister of Labour</a>. He later held the role of <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a> and in 1963, was made <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Trade" title="President of the Board of Trade">President of the Board of Trade</a> by <a href="/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home" title="Alec Douglas-Home">Alec Douglas-Home</a>. After the Conservatives were defeated at the <a href="/wiki/1964_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1964 United Kingdom general election">1964 election</a>, Heath <a href="/wiki/1965_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="1965 Conservative Party leadership election">was elected</a> as Leader of the Conservative Party in 1965, becoming <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(United_Kingdom)" title="Leader of the Opposition (United Kingdom)">Leader of the Opposition</a>. Although he led the Conservatives to a landslide defeat at the <a href="/wiki/1966_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1966 United Kingdom general election">1966 election</a>, he remained in the leadership, and at the <a href="/wiki/1970_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1970 United Kingdom general election">1970 election</a> led his party to an unexpected victory.
</p><p>During his time as prime minister, Heath oversaw the <a href="/wiki/Decimal_Day" title="Decimal Day">decimalisation of British coinage</a> in 1971, and in 1972 he led the reformation of <a href="/wiki/Local_government_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Local government in the United Kingdom">local government</a>, significantly reducing the number of local authorities and creating several new metropolitan counties, much of which remains to this day. Perhaps Heath's most prominent achievement came in 1973, when he led the United Kingdom into membership of the <a href="/wiki/European_Communities" title="European Communities">European Communities</a> popularly known as entry to the Common Market (which would later become the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>) as a <a href="/wiki/Member_state_of_the_European_Union" title="Member state of the European Union">member state</a>. Heath had always been a strong supporter of British membership of the EC, and after winning the decisive vote in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> by 356 to 244 to join, he led the negotiations that culminated in the UK's <a href="/wiki/Accession_of_the_United_Kingdom_to_the_European_Communities" title="Accession of the United Kingdom to the European Communities">entry into the EC</a> on 1 January 1973.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> According to biographer <a href="/wiki/John_Campbell_(biographer)" title="John Campbell (biographer)">John Campbell</a>, Heath regarded this as his personal "finest hour".<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Heath's time as prime minister also coincided with the height of <a href="/wiki/The_Troubles" title="The Troubles">the Troubles</a> in Northern Ireland, with his approval of <a href="/wiki/Operation_Demetrius" title="Operation Demetrius">internment without trial</a> and subsequent suspension of the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Northern_Ireland" title="Parliament of Northern Ireland">Stormont Parliament</a> seeing the imposition of <a href="/wiki/Direct_rule_(Northern_Ireland)" title="Direct rule (Northern Ireland)">direct British rule</a>. Unofficial talks with <a href="/wiki/Provisional_Irish_Republican_Army" title="Provisional Irish Republican Army">Provisional Irish Republican Army</a> delegates were unsuccessful, as was the <a href="/wiki/Sunningdale_Agreement" title="Sunningdale Agreement">Sunningdale Agreement</a> of 1973, which led the MPs of the <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Unionist_Party" title="Ulster Unionist Party">Ulster Unionist Party</a> to withdraw from the Conservative whip. Heath also tried to reform British trade unionism with the <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Relations_Act_1971" title="Industrial Relations Act 1971">Industrial Relations Act</a>, and hoped to deregulate the economy and make a transfer from <a href="/wiki/Direct_tax" title="Direct tax">direct</a> to <a href="/wiki/Indirect_tax" title="Indirect tax">indirect taxation</a>. However, a <a href="/wiki/Three-Day_Week" title="Three-Day Week">miners' strike</a> at the start of 1974 severely damaged the Government, causing the implementation of the <a href="/wiki/Three-Day_Week" title="Three-Day Week">Three-Day Week</a> to conserve energy. Attempting to resolve the situation, Heath called an <a href="/wiki/February_1974_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="February 1974 United Kingdom general election">election</a> for February 1974, attempting to obtain a mandate to face down the miners' wage demands, but this instead resulted in a <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a>, with the Conservatives losing their majority. Despite gaining fewer votes, the Labour Party won four more seats, and Heath resigned as Prime Minister on 4 March after talks with the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal Party</a> to form a coalition government were unsuccessful.
</p><p>After losing a <a href="/wiki/October_1974_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="October 1974 United Kingdom general election">second successive election</a> in October 1974, Heath insisted he would continue as leader, but in January 1975, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a> announced she would <a href="/wiki/1975_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="1975 Conservative Party leadership election">challenge Heath</a> for the leadership, and on 4 February, she narrowly outpolled him in the first round. Heath chose to resign the leadership rather than contest the second round. Heath returned to the backbenches, where he would remain until 2001. In 1975, he played a major role in the <a href="/wiki/1975_United_Kingdom_European_Communities_membership_referendum" title="1975 United Kingdom European Communities membership referendum">referendum</a> on British membership of the EC, campaigning for the eventually successful "Yes" vote to remain in the Community. Heath would later become an embittered critic of Thatcher during her time as prime minister, speaking and writing against the policies of <a href="/wiki/Thatcherism" title="Thatcherism">Thatcherism</a>. Following the <a href="/wiki/1992_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1992 United Kingdom general election">1992 election</a>, he became <a href="/wiki/Father_of_the_House_(United_Kingdom)" title="Father of the House (United Kingdom)">Father of the House</a>, until his retirement from the Commons in 2001. He died in 2005, aged 89.
</p><p>Heath is one of four British prime ministers never to have married. He has been described by the BBC as "the first working-class meritocrat" to become Conservative leader in "the party's modern history" and "a <a href="/wiki/One-nation_conservatism" title="One-nation conservatism">One Nation Tory</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Disraeli</a> tradition who rejected the <a href="/wiki/Laissez-faire_capitalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Laissez-faire capitalism">laissez-faire capitalism</a> that Thatcher would enthusiastically endorse."<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Heath was born at 54 Albion Road, Broadstairs, Kent, on 9 July 1916, the son of William George Heath (1888–1976), a carpenter who built <a href="/wiki/Airframe" title="Airframe">airframes</a> for <a href="/wiki/Vickers_Limited" title="Vickers Limited">Vickers</a> during the First World War, and was subsequently employed as a builder<sup id="cite_ref-Britain,_Arthur_Marwick_1996,_p._158_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britain,_Arthur_Marwick_1996,_p._158-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and Edith Anne Heath (<i>née</i> Pantony; 1888–1951), a lady's maid.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> His father was later a successful small businessman after taking over a building and decorating firm. Heath's paternal grandfather had run a small dairy business, and when that failed worked as a porter at <a href="/wiki/Broadstairs_railway_station" title="Broadstairs railway station">Broadstairs Station</a> on the Southern Railway.<sup id="cite_ref-Britain,_Arthur_Marwick_1996,_p._158_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britain,_Arthur_Marwick_1996,_p._158-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Edward was four years old when his younger brother, John, was born; there was no question that Edward was the "favoured brother".<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Heath was known as "Teddy" as a young man.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He was educated at <a href="/wiki/Chatham_House_Grammar_School" title="Chatham House Grammar School">Chatham House Grammar School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ramsgate" title="Ramsgate">Ramsgate</a>, and in 1935 with the aid of a county scholarship he went up to study at <a href="/wiki/Balliol_College,_Oxford" title="Balliol College, Oxford">Balliol College, Oxford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In later years, Heath's peculiar accent, with its "strangulated" vowel sounds, combined with his non-Standard pronunciation of "l" as "w" and "out" as "eout", was satirised by <a href="/wiki/Monty_Python" title="Monty Python">Monty Python</a> in the audio sketch "<a href="/wiki/Teach_Yourself_Heath" title="Teach Yourself Heath">Teach Yourself Heath</a>" (released on a 7" flexi-disc single included with initial copies of their 1972 LP <i><a href="/wiki/Monty_Python%27s_Previous_Record" title="Monty Python&#39;s Previous Record">Monty Python's Previous Record</a></i>).<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Heath's biographer <a href="/wiki/John_Campbell_(biographer)" title="John Campbell (biographer)">John Campbell</a> speculates that his speech, unlike that of his father and younger brother, who both spoke with Kent accents, must have undergone "drastic alteration on encountering Oxford", although retaining elements of Kent speech.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Oxford">Oxford</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Oxford">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>A talented musician, Heath won the college's <a href="/wiki/Organ_scholar" title="Organ scholar">organ scholarship</a> in his first term (he had previously tried for the organ scholarships at <a href="/wiki/St_Catharine%27s_College,_Cambridge" title="St Catharine&#39;s College, Cambridge">St Catharine's College, Cambridge</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Keble_College,_Oxford" title="Keble College, Oxford">Keble College, Oxford</a>) which enabled him to stay at the university for a fourth year; he eventually graduated with a <a href="/wiki/British_undergraduate_degree_classification" title="British undergraduate degree classification">Second Class Honours</a> BA in <a href="/wiki/Philosophy,_politics_and_economics" title="Philosophy, politics and economics">Philosophy, politics and economics</a> in 1939.
</p><p>While at university Heath became active in Conservative Party politics. On the key political issue of the day, foreign policy, he opposed the Conservative-dominated government of the day ever more openly. His first Paper Speech (i.e. a major speech listed on the <a href="/wiki/Order_Paper" title="Order Paper">Order Paper</a> along with the visiting guest speakers) at the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_Union" title="Oxford Union">Oxford Union</a>, in 1936, was in opposition to the <a href="/wiki/Appeasement" title="Appeasement">appeasement</a> of Germany by returning her colonies, confiscated during the First World War.
</p><p>In June 1937 he was elected President of the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University_Conservative_Association" title="Oxford University Conservative Association">Oxford University Conservative Association</a> as a pro-<a href="/wiki/Second_Spanish_Republic" title="Second Spanish Republic">Spanish Republic</a> candidate, in opposition to the pro-<a href="/wiki/Francisco_Franco" title="Francisco Franco">Franco</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Stokes_(Conservative_politician)" title="John Stokes (Conservative politician)">John Stokes</a> (himself later a Conservative MP). In 1937–38 Heath was chairman of the national <a href="/wiki/Federation_of_Conservative_Students" title="Federation of Conservative Students">Federation of University Conservative Associations</a>, and in the same year (his third at university) he was Secretary and then Librarian of the Oxford Union. At the end of the year he was defeated for the Presidency of the Oxford Union by another Balliol candidate, Alan Wood, on the issue of whether the <a href="/wiki/Chamberlain_war_ministry" title="Chamberlain war ministry">Chamberlain government</a> should give way to a left-wing <a href="/wiki/Popular_Front_(UK)" title="Popular Front (UK)">Popular Front</a>. On that occasion, Heath supported the government.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his final year Heath was President of Balliol College <a href="/wiki/Junior_Common_Room" class="mw-redirect" title="Junior Common Room">Junior Common Room</a>, an office held in subsequent years by his near-contemporaries <a href="/wiki/Denis_Healey" title="Denis Healey">Denis Healey</a>, who would become a lifelong friend and political rival<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Roy_Jenkins" title="Roy Jenkins">Roy Jenkins</a>, and as such was invited to support the Master of Balliol <a href="/wiki/Sandie_Lindsay,_1st_Baron_Lindsay_of_Birker" title="Sandie Lindsay, 1st Baron Lindsay of Birker">Alexander Lindsay</a>, who stood as an anti-appeasement 'Independent Progressive' candidate against the official Conservative candidate, <a href="/wiki/Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_of_St_Marylebone" title="Quintin Hogg, Baron Hailsham of St Marylebone">Quintin Hogg</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/1938_Oxford_by-election" title="1938 Oxford by-election">1938 Oxford by-election</a>.
</p><p>Heath, who had himself applied to be the Conservative candidate for the by-election,<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> accused the government in an October Union Debate of "turning all four cheeks" to <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a>, and was elected as <a href="/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_Oxford_Union" title="List of presidents of the Oxford Union">President of the Oxford Union</a> in November 1938, sponsored by Balliol, after winning the Presidential Debate that "This House has No Confidence in the National Government as presently constituted". He was thus President in <a href="/wiki/Hilary_term" title="Hilary term">Hilary term</a> 1939; the visiting <a href="/wiki/Leo_Amery" title="Leo Amery">Leo Amery</a> described him in his diaries as "a pleasant youth".
</p><p>As an undergraduate, Heath travelled widely in Europe. His opposition to appeasement was nourished by his witnessing first-hand a <a href="/wiki/Nuremberg_rallies" title="Nuremberg rallies">Nuremberg Rally</a> in 1937, where he met leading Nazis <a href="/wiki/Hermann_G%C3%B6ring" title="Hermann Göring">Hermann Göring</a>, <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Goebbels" title="Joseph Goebbels">Joseph Goebbels</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_Himmler" title="Heinrich Himmler">Heinrich Himmler</a> at an <a href="/wiki/Schutzstaffel" title="Schutzstaffel">SS</a> cocktail party. He later described Himmler as "the most evil man I have ever met".<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He was in Germany for two months to learn German but did not keep up any fluency in the language in later life.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In 1938 he visited <a href="/wiki/Barcelona" title="Barcelona">Barcelona</a>, then under attack from Spanish Nationalist forces during the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War" title="Spanish Civil War">Spanish Civil War</a>. On one occasion a car in which he was travelling came under machine-gun fire, while on another a bomb hit his hotel whilst he was observing an air raid from outside.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> In the summer of 1939, accompanied by his Jewish friend <a href="/wiki/Madron_Seligman" title="Madron Seligman">Madron Seligman</a>, he travelled to <a href="/wiki/Gda%C5%84sk" title="Gdańsk">Danzig</a> and <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>. They made the return journey by hitchhiking and rail across Germany through mobilising troops, returning to Britain just before the declaration of war.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_World_War">Second World War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Second World War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Heath spent late 1939 and early 1940 on a debating tour of the United States before being called up. On 22 March 1941, he received an emergency commission as a <a href="/wiki/Second_lieutenant" title="Second lieutenant">second lieutenant</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Artillery" title="Royal Artillery">Royal Artillery</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> During the war he initially served with heavy anti-aircraft guns around <a href="/wiki/Liverpool" title="Liverpool">Liverpool</a> (which suffered <a href="/wiki/Liverpool_Blitz" title="Liverpool Blitz">heavy German bombing</a> in May 1941) and by early 1942 was regimental <a href="/wiki/Adjutant" title="Adjutant">adjutant</a>, with the war substantive rank of <a href="/wiki/Captain_(British_Army_and_Royal_Marines)" title="Captain (British Army and Royal Marines)">captain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto1_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Heath participated as an adjutant in the <a href="/wiki/Normandy_landings" title="Normandy landings">Normandy landings</a>, where he met <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Schumann" title="Maurice Schumann">Maurice Schumann</a>, French Foreign Minister under <a href="/wiki/Georges_Pompidou" title="Georges Pompidou">Pompidou</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> As a temporary major commanding a battery of his own, he provided artillery support during the Allied campaigns in France and Germany in 1944–45, for which he received a <a href="/wiki/Mentioned_in_dispatches" title="Mentioned in dispatches">mention in despatches</a> on 8 November 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-auto1_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Heath later remarked that, although he did not personally kill anybody, as the British forces advanced he saw the devastation caused by his unit's artillery bombardments. In September 1945 he commanded a firing squad that executed a Polish soldier convicted of rape and murder.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_British_Empire" title="Order of the British Empire">Member of the Order of the British Empire</a>, Military Division (MBE) on 24 January 1946.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> He was demobilised in August 1946 and promoted to the substantive rank of <a href="/wiki/Lieutenant_colonel_(United_Kingdom)" title="Lieutenant colonel (United Kingdom)">lieutenant-colonel</a> on 1 May 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Heath joined the <a href="/wiki/Honourable_Artillery_Company" title="Honourable Artillery Company">Honourable Artillery Company</a> as a lieutenant-colonel on 1 September 1951,<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> in which he remained active throughout the 1950s, rising to commanding officer of the Second Battalion; a portrait of him in full dress uniform still hangs in the HAC's Long Room. In April 1971, as prime minister, he wore his lieutenant-colonel's insignia to inspect troops.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-war.2C_1945.E2.80.931950"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-war,_1945–1950">Post-war, 1945–1950</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Post-war, 1945–1950">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Before the war, Heath had won a scholarship to <a href="/wiki/Gray%27s_Inn" title="Gray&#39;s Inn">Gray's Inn</a> and had begun making preparations for a career at the Bar, but after the war he was placed in joint top position in the <a href="/wiki/Civil_service_examination" class="mw-redirect" title="Civil service examination">civil service examinations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He then became a civil servant in the Ministry of Civil Aviation (he was disappointed not to be posted to the Treasury, but declined an offer to join the Foreign Office, fearing that foreign postings might prevent him from entering politics).<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Heath joined a team under <a href="/wiki/Alison_Munro" title="Alison Munro">Alison Munro</a> tasked with drawing up a scheme for British airports using some of the many Second World War <a href="/wiki/Royal_Air_Force" title="Royal Air Force">RAF</a> bases, and was specifically charged with planning the home counties. Years later she attributed his evident enthusiasm for Maplin Airport to this work. Then much to the surprise of civil service colleagues, he sought adoption as the prospective parliamentary candidate for <a href="/wiki/Bexley" title="Bexley">Bexley</a> and resigned in November 1947.
</p><p>After working as news editor of the <i><a href="/wiki/Church_Times" title="Church Times">Church Times</a></i> from February 1948 to September 1949,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Heath worked as a management trainee at the <a href="/wiki/Merchant_bank" title="Merchant bank">merchant bankers</a> <a href="/wiki/Brown,_Shipley_%26_Co." class="mw-redirect" title="Brown, Shipley &amp; Co.">Brown, Shipley &amp; Co.</a> until his election as <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">Member of Parliament</a> (MP) for <a href="/wiki/Bexley_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Bexley (UK Parliament constituency)">Bexley</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1950_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1950 United Kingdom general election">February 1950 general election</a>. In the election he defeated an old contemporary from the Oxford Union, <a href="/wiki/Ashley_Bramall" title="Ashley Bramall">Ashley Bramall</a>, by a margin of 133 votes.
</p>
<h2><span id="Member_of_Parliament_.281950.E2.80.931965.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament_(1950–1965)">Member of Parliament (1950–1965)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Member of Parliament (1950–1965)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg/170px-Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg/255px-Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg/340px-Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg 2x" data-file-width="581" data-file-height="724" /></a><figcaption>Official portrait, 1960</figcaption></figure>
<p>Heath made his <a href="/wiki/Maiden_speech" title="Maiden speech">maiden speech</a> in the House of Commons on 26 June 1950, in which he appealed to the Labour government to participate in the <a href="/wiki/Schuman_Plan" class="mw-redirect" title="Schuman Plan">Schuman Plan</a>. As MP for Bexley, he gave enthusiastic speeches in support of the young candidate for neighbouring <a href="/wiki/Dartford_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Dartford (UK Parliament constituency)">Dartford</a>, Margaret Roberts, later Margaret Thatcher.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was appointed as an <a href="/wiki/Whip_(politics)" title="Whip (politics)">opposition whip</a> by <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> in February 1951. He remained in the whips' office after the Conservatives won the <a href="/wiki/1951_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1951 United Kingdom general election">1951 general election</a>, rising rapidly to Joint Deputy Chief Whip, Deputy Chief Whip and, in December 1955, <a href="/wiki/Chief_Whip_of_the_Conservative_Party" title="Chief Whip of the Conservative Party">Government Chief Whip</a> under <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden" title="Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a>. Journalist <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Wheatcroft" title="Geoffrey Wheatcroft">Geoffrey Wheatcroft</a> has observed that "Of all government jobs, this requires firmness and fairness allied to tact and patience and Heath's ascent seems baffling in hindsight".<sup id="cite_ref-guardian.co.uk_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardian.co.uk-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Due to the convention that whips do not speak in Parliament, Heath managed to keep out of the controversy over the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Crisis" title="Suez Crisis">Suez Crisis</a>. On the announcement of Eden's resignation, Heath submitted a report on the opinions of the Conservative MPs regarding Eden's possible successors. This report favoured <a href="/wiki/Harold_Macmillan" title="Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a> and helped to secure Macmillan the premiership in January 1957. Macmillan later appointed Heath Minister of Labour, a Cabinet Minister—as Chief Whip Heath had attended Cabinet, but had not been formally a member—after winning the <a href="/wiki/1959_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1959 United Kingdom general election">October 1959 election</a>.
</p><p>In 1960 Macmillan appointed Heath <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a> with responsibility for the negotiations to secure the UK's first attempt to join the <a href="/wiki/European_Communities" title="European Communities">European Communities</a> (or <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Community" title="European Economic Community">Common Market</a>, as it was then more widely known). After extensive negotiations, involving detailed agreements about the UK's agricultural trade with <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth</a> countries such as <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>, British entry was vetoed by the French President, <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">Charles de Gaulle</a>, at a press conference in January 1963 – much to the disappointment of Heath, who was a firm supporter of European common market membership for the United Kingdom. He oversaw a successful application when serving as prime minister a decade later.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After this setback, a major humiliation for Macmillan's foreign policy, Heath was not a contender for the party leadership on Macmillan's retirement in October 1963. Under prime minister Sir <a href="/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home" title="Alec Douglas-Home">Alec Douglas-Home</a> he was <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Trade" title="President of the Board of Trade">President of the Board of Trade</a> and Secretary of State for Industry, Trade and Regional Development, and oversaw the abolition of <a href="/wiki/Resale_price_maintenance" title="Resale price maintenance">retail price maintenance</a>.
</p>
<h2><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_.281965.E2.80.931970.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_(1965–1970)">Leader of the Opposition (1965–1970)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition (1965–1970)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Golda_Meir_and_Edward_Heath.jpg/400px-Golda_Meir_and_Edward_Heath.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3066" data-file-height="2052" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">Heath with Israeli prime minister <a href="/wiki/Golda_Meir" title="Golda Meir">Golda Meir</a> in 1969</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>After the Conservative Party lost the <a href="/wiki/1964_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1964 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1964</a>, the defeated Home changed the party leadership rules to allow for a ballot by MPs and then resigned. The following year, Heath—who was <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Shadow Chancellor of the Exchequer">Shadow Chancellor</a> at the time, and had recently won favourable publicity for leading the fight against Labour's <a href="/wiki/Finance_Act" title="Finance Act">Finance Bill</a>—unexpectedly won the <a href="/wiki/1965_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="1965 Conservative Party leadership election">party's leadership contest</a>, gaining 150 votes to <a href="/wiki/Reginald_Maudling" title="Reginald Maudling">Reginald Maudling</a>'s 133 and <a href="/wiki/Enoch_Powell" title="Enoch Powell">Enoch Powell</a>'s 15.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Heath became the Conservatives' youngest leader and retained office following the party's defeat in the <a href="/wiki/1966_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1966 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1966</a>.
</p><p>In April 1968, <a href="/wiki/Enoch_Powell" title="Enoch Powell">Enoch Powell</a> made his controversial <a href="/wiki/Rivers_of_Blood_speech" title="Rivers of Blood speech">"Rivers of Blood" speech</a>, which criticised <a href="/wiki/Modern_immigration_to_the_United_Kingdom" title="Modern immigration to the United Kingdom">immigration to the United Kingdom</a>. Soon afterwards, Heath telephoned Margaret Thatcher to inform her that he was going to sack Powell from the <a href="/wiki/Shadow_cabinet" title="Shadow cabinet">Shadow cabinet</a>; she recalled that she "really thought that it was better to let things cool down for the present rather than heighten the crisis". The next day, Heath sacked Powell. Several Conservatives on the right protested against Powell's sacking.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> According to Heath, he never spoke to Powell again.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1970_election">1970 election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: 1970 election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>With another general election approaching in 1970 a Conservative policy document emerged from the <a href="/wiki/De_Vere_Selsdon_Estate" title="De Vere Selsdon Estate">Selsdon Park Hotel</a> that offered free-market&#8211;oriented policies as solutions to the country's unemployment and inflation problems.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Heath stated that the Selsdon weekend only reaffirmed policies that had actually been evolving since he became leader of the Conservative Party. The Labour prime minister, <a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a>, thought the document a vote-loser and dubbed it the product of <i>Selsdon Man</i> – after the supposedly prehistoric <i><a href="/wiki/Piltdown_Man" title="Piltdown Man">Piltdown Man</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> – to portray it as reactionary. Heath's Conservative Party won the <a href="/wiki/1970_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1970 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1970</a> with 330 seats to Labour's 287. The new cabinet included the future prime minister Margaret Thatcher (Education and Science), <a href="/wiki/William_Whitelaw,_1st_Viscount_Whitelaw" title="William Whitelaw, 1st Viscount Whitelaw">William Whitelaw</a> (Leader of the House of Commons) and the former prime minister <a href="/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home" title="Alec Douglas-Home">Alec Douglas-Home</a> (Foreign and Commonwealth Affairs).<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_minister_.281970.E2.80.931974.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister_(1970–1974)">Prime minister (1970–1974)<span class="anchor" id="Premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Prime minister (1970–1974)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Heath_ministry" title="Heath ministry">Heath ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Richard_Nixon_and_Prime_Minister_Edward_Heath_shake_hands_upon_Heath%27s_Arrival_at_the_White_House.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/President_Richard_Nixon_and_Prime_Minister_Edward_Heath_shake_hands_upon_Heath%27s_Arrival_at_the_White_House.jpg/260px-President_Richard_Nixon_and_Prime_Minister_Edward_Heath_shake_hands_upon_Heath%27s_Arrival_at_the_White_House.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="174" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/President_Richard_Nixon_and_Prime_Minister_Edward_Heath_shake_hands_upon_Heath%27s_Arrival_at_the_White_House.jpg/390px-President_Richard_Nixon_and_Prime_Minister_Edward_Heath_shake_hands_upon_Heath%27s_Arrival_at_the_White_House.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/President_Richard_Nixon_and_Prime_Minister_Edward_Heath_shake_hands_upon_Heath%27s_Arrival_at_the_White_House.jpg/520px-President_Richard_Nixon_and_Prime_Minister_Edward_Heath_shake_hands_upon_Heath%27s_Arrival_at_the_White_House.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5775" data-file-height="3871" /></a><figcaption>Heath with US president <a href="/wiki/Richard_Nixon" title="Richard Nixon">Richard Nixon</a> at the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> in 1973</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Welfare_state">Welfare state</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Welfare state">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During Heath's first year in office, higher charges were introduced for benefits of the welfare state such as school meals, spectacles, dentistry, and prescriptions. Entitlement to State Sickness Benefit was also changed so that it would only be paid after the first three days of sickness.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In 1971 a series of measures was introduced designed to limit the availability of supplementary benefit. In particular, as noted by one study, "tax rebates and strike pay were treated as resources, subject to a £4 disregard."<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> From April 1972 a man returning from work after being on strike had to pay back any supplementary benefit received to tide him over during the first 15 days at work before he got his pay. In February 1973, the rules about help in urgent cases were made more stringent.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> As a result of the squeeze in the education budget, the provision of <a href="/wiki/Education_Act_1944#free_school_milk" title="Education Act 1944">free school milk</a> was ended for 8- to 11-year-olds (it had already been ended for older children by Harold Wilson); the tabloid press christened Margaret Thatcher, the then Education Secretary as "Margaret Thatcher: Milk Snatcher".<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Despite these measures, the Heath government encouraged a significant increase in welfare spending, and Thatcher blocked Macleod's other posthumous education policy: the abolition of the <a href="/wiki/Open_University" title="Open University">Open University</a>, which had recently been founded by the preceding Labour government.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In addition, despite the end of cheap milk provision however, 700,000 school children remained entitled to free milk. The Legal Advice and Assistance Act 1972 provided for better facilities for advice and assistance at an early stage in a dispute.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> The Social Needs (Grants) Act 9NI) 1970 "made provision for the authorisation of the payment of grants towards expenditure incurred due to special social need in urban areas."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In January 1973, the Criminal Justice Act 1972 came into effect in England and Wales. It extended the court’s power “to order an offender to pay compensation for personal injury, loss, or damage.”<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> In June 1973 a new lower rate of attendance allowance was introduced for those aged 16 to 64, while lower rates "for the aged under 15 and those over 65 were introduced on 1 October and 3 December respectively."<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Another change allowed for small claims of less than £75 to be dealt with in a less formal manner within the County Court system. Rules of evidence and requirements of legal representation were relaxed to make it viable to claim such small sums under the County Court Act 1959 as amended by the Administration of Justice Act (E&amp;W) 1973.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Provision was made under the <a href="/w/index.php?title=National_Insurance_(Old_Persons%27_and_Widows%27_Pensions_and_Attendances_Allowances)_Act_1970&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="National Insurance (Old Persons&#39; and Widows&#39; Pensions and Attendances Allowances) Act 1970 (page does not exist)">National Insurance (Old Persons' and Widows' Pensions and Attendances Allowances) Act 1970</a> for pensions to be paid to old people who had been excluded from the pre-1948 pension schemes and were accordingly excluded from the comprehensive scheme that was introduced in 1948. About 100,000 people were affected by this change, half of whom were receiving <a href="/wiki/Supplementary_Benefit" title="Supplementary Benefit">Supplementary Benefit</a> under the social security scheme. The Act also made improvements to the Widow's Pension scheme by introducing a scale that started at 30 shillings a week for women widowed at the age of 40 and rose to the full rate of £5 at the age of 50.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Considerable support was provided for nursery school building, and a long-term capital investment programme in school building was launched. A <a href="/wiki/Family_Fund" title="Family Fund">Family Fund</a> was set up to assist families with children who had congenital conditions,<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> while new benefits were introduced benefiting hundreds of thousands of disabled persons whose disabilities had been caused neither by war nor by industrial injury. An <a href="/wiki/Attendance_Allowance" title="Attendance Allowance">Attendance Allowance</a> was introduced for those needing care at home, together with <a href="/wiki/Invalidity_Benefit" title="Invalidity Benefit">Invalidity Benefit</a> for the long-term sick, while a higher Child Allowance was made available where invalidity allowance was paid. Widow's Benefits were introduced for those aged between forty and fifty years of age, improved subsidies for <a href="/wiki/Slum_clearance_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Slum clearance in the United Kingdom">slum clearance</a> were made available, while Rent Allowances were introduced for private tenants.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In April 1971, the right to education was given to all children with Down's syndrome for the first time.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The school leaving age was raised to 16,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> while <a href="/wiki/Family_Income_Supplement" title="Family Income Supplement">Family Income Supplement</a> was introduced to boost the incomes of low-income earners.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Families who received this benefit were exempted from <a href="/wiki/Prescription_charges" title="Prescription charges">NHS charges</a> while the children in such families were eligible for <a href="/wiki/School_meal#United_Kingdom" title="School meal">free school meals</a>. <a href="/wiki/Social_pension" title="Social pension">Non-contributory pensions</a> were also introduced for all persons aged eighty and above,<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> while the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Social_Security_Act_1973&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Social Security Act 1973 (page does not exist)">Social Security Act 1973</a> was passed which introduced benefit indexation in the United Kingdom for the first time by index-linking benefits to prices to maintain their real value.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The National Insurance Act of 1971 introduced a special addition with no means test for all pensioners over 80, while in 1972 a £10 Christmas bonus was introduced.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Annual upratings for all pensioners was also introduced.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> From October 1973 onwards a higher ' long - term rate ' of supplementary benefit "was paid to those who had received supplementary benefit for a continuous period."<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> That same year, a basic short-term rates were introduced for everyone else; the short-term sick and unemployed in the main.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> From 1972 onwards, "while there is no statutory commitment to this effect, the same cash increases have been made in the leading supplementary benefit scale rates as in their national insurance counterparts." In 1973, a statutory commitment to uprate national insurance benefits in line with prices was introduced in 1973.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> The Industrial Relations Act of 1971 gave employees "the right, depending on a minimum qualifying period, to appeal to an industrial tribunal, where compensation for unfair dismissal could be awarded based on length of service,"<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> and in 1972 preservation of accrued pension benefits after 5 or more years' service was introduced.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The Legal Advice and Assistance Act 1972 set up a Green Form scheme under which advice of up to two hours, free of charge to the poorest or at reduced rates to those of modest means, became available.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> The Housing Finance Act 1972 "introduced a national formula for rent increases in the public sector and a mandatory system of local rent and rate rebates that survived subsequent legislation."<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> The Supplementary Benefit (Determination of Requirements) Regulations 1972 increased the rent addition for non-householders and removed the present age qualification.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> The Students' Dependents' Allowances Regulations 1973 enabled the Secretary of State "to pay allowances to students attending first degree university etc. courses and courses for the training of teachers."<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> A social security amendment of 1973 provided for allowing the Supplementary Benefits Commission "to make payments to cover funeral expenses without having regard to the powers of local authorities, so that the commission will not be inhibited by the fact that local authorities have certain powers concerning the disposal of bodies."<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Subsequently, the Commission "revised its policy on payments towards funeral expenses . Claimants are no longer advised to ask the Local Authority to make funeral arrangements, and the Commission will consider paying towards the costs of a private funeral under its powers to meet exceptional needs."<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Redundant Mineworkers and Concessionary Coal (Payments Schemes) Order 1973 provided for three new types of benefits, including "lump sums based on length of service for men redundant between age 35 and 55, and for men over 55 who due to lack of service in the industry do not qualify for basic benefit; a new benefit equivalent to the current rate of unemployment benefit to be paid to men when they have exhausted their 156 weeks of basic benefit until they reach age 65; and concessionary coal benefits for men made redundant between age 55 and 60, and certain men redundant over age 60. (Most men redundant over age 60 already receive concessionary coal under existing arrangements.) In addition, "The amount of benefit which a beneficiary may retain if he obtains other employment has also been increased."<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> The Employment and Training Act 1973 included various provisions. For instance, as specified in the text of the Act, "The Secretary of State may— pay to any person appointed in pursuance of the preceding paragraph such subsistence and travelling allowances and such compensation for loss of remunerative time as the Secretary of State may determine with the approval of the Minister for the Civil Service." In addition, "An industrial training board may— pay maintenance and  travelling allowances to persons attending courses provided or approved by the board&#160;; make grants or loans to persons providing courses or other facilities approved by the board, to persons who make studies for the purpose of providing such courses or facilities and to persons who maintain arrangements to provide such courses or facilities which are not for the time being in use&#160;; pay fees to persons providing further education in respect of persons who receive it in association with their training in courses provided or approved by the board; make payments to persons in connection with arrangements under which they or employees of theirs make use of courses or other facilities provided or approved by the board."<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Redundant Mineworkers (Payments Scheme) Order 1972 was intended to be make available for 3 years "to any miner who is made redundant after he has reached 55 to help him to adjust to the new situation and to the difficulties which confront him." It included a number of provisions for miners and their dependents including the addition of cost of living increments, a basic scheme benefit, an increase from £3 to £6 a week to the benefit "which can be kept if a redundant mineworker finds a new job and enters new employment, because if he did he would probably incur income tax, perhaps National Insurance contributions, travelling expenses and other expenses which might well have exceeded the £3 which he would have been able to draw if he had continued to draw that from the redundant mineworkers payment scheme." The rent allowance which is payable to a redundant mineworker was made transferable to a new house if he were to move his home, the rules regarding the offsetting of State and other coal industry benefits against the total of the mineworkers' redundancy payment benefit were eased, and the scheme was changed "so that in the event of a second redundancy—if a miner is made redundant a second time—his earnings are computed on the wages paid in the year immediately before the second redundancy as opposed to his previous level of earnings." Also, "these last three concessions will apply to men who are in the existing scheme, not only to miners who might be made redundant in future, but to all those who are part of the existing scheme at the present time." The scheme also provided that the general increases in benefits, such as the special hardship allowance and workmen's compensation supplementation would not be offset as at present, and also provided for increases in benefits paid to men on workmen's compensation as a result of becoming total or major incapacity cases, award of special hardship allowance in respect of an accident or disease sustained after redundancies. In addition, "any general increase in unemployment benefit between 6th April, 1972, and 6th April, 1973, will be offset against the benefit of men made redundant after 6th April, 1973." Also, "the maximum amount of benefit that can be kept by those who get a job is increased from £3 to £6" weekly, and "the table of basic benefit appropriate to the various ranges of pre-redundancy earnings set out in Appendix 4 has been recalculated so that the basic benefit, plus the 1971 rate of unemployment benefit for a man and one adult dependant, equals, after tax and on average over the three years, 90 per cent. of previous take-home pay." Other provisions included service in the coal industry "before nationalisation can now count towards the 10 qualifying years" and that women "who are not paying the full National Insurance stamp will be able to get some benefit despite not being eligible for unemployment benefit." In addition, "days of absence from home which disqualify a person from unemployment benefit, and also from 72 scheme benefit, will be counted as days rather than one day in a week resulting in a whole week's disqualification as at present." Also, "a man made redundant within the terms of the scheme and reemployed in the coal industry for more than a year and then made redundant a second time can choose on which of the two pre-redundancy earnings he wishes his benefit to be calculated." In addition, "the requirement that in order to continue getting the rent allowance a person must continue to live in the same house in which he was living when he was made redundant, and that this house must continue to be owned by the board, is removed." Also, "the relevant tax year on which pre-redundancy earnings are based will now be the previous tax year for everybody, instead of being two years ago for those made redundant during April."<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Also, as noted by one MP, "Following the introduction of the first redundant mineworkers payments scheme in 1968, which provided benefits for men aged 55 or over on redundancy who otherwise satisfied the requirements of the scheme, there was pressure to enhance the terms available to enable such men to receive concessionary coal as though they had reached normal retirement age. The Government responded to this pressure in the Coal Industry Act 1973, amending the power to make schemes benefiting redundant mineworkers in the 1967 Act." Also in 1973,  the first concessionary coal payments scheme was introduced by order, which provided for reimbursement to the National Coal Board "of the full cost of concessionary coal to men aged 55 to 59 who qualified for benefits under the redundant mineworkers payments scheme from 14 December 1969, and half the cost of providing concessionary coal to such men age 60 to 65."<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A number of new superannuation provisions were introduced for various categories of workers.  For those in the NHS, this included years and days used in benefit calculations instead of years and half years, the introduction of practitioner dynamising introduced i.e. 1.4% pensions, best of the last 3 years Total Superannuable Remuneration introduced instead of average of last 3 years, 3 x lump sum introduced for post 24.03.1972 service (married men), the introduction of a half rate widow's pension (was previously 1/3), a new rate of Child Allowance, a reduction in the qualifying period for pensions from 10 years to 5 years, the abolition of the qualifying period for Death Gratuities (DG),  the introduction of double incapacity build up for service between 5 years and 9 years 364 days (or to age 65 if less), the introduction of 3 months initial widows pension, the introduction of widows limited pension (for 3, 4.5 or 6 months) for those widows who do not have title to continuing widows pensions, the introduction of a Limited Child Allowance, the introduction of automatic payment of benefits at age 70, the introduction of Added Years purchase, "Pensions Increase Supplement may apply"  from January 1973 to October 1975, the extension of service to take account of untaken annual leave introduced for ancillary staff, and "Part time service may be superannuable provided half or more of the w/t basic is worked."<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The Pensions (Increase) Act of 1971 provided for regular increases in public service pensions by statutory instrument. The Act was later amended in 1972 and 1974 "to lower the minimum qualifying age for pensions increase from 60 to 55 and to provide for all pensions paid to widows of scheme members to be increased."<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Superannuation Act of 1972 provided for new arrangements for premature retirement on grounds of limited efficiency,<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> granted civil servants rights to their pensions<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> and established the Principal Civil Service Pension Scheme, the Civil Service Additional Voluntary Contribution Scheme (CSAVCS), the Civil Service Compensation Scheme (CSCS) and the Civil Service Injury Benefits Scheme (CSIBS).<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The Local Government (Retirement of Chief Officers) Regulations 1973 provided "for persons identified as chief officers and deputy chief officers of local authorities, and certain other persons affected by local government reorganisation, to elect for early retirement on enhanced pension terms and thereby forgo any right to claim compensation which might otherwise have been exercised under section 259 of the Local Government Act 1972."<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Section 27 of the Water Act 1973 empowered water authorities to establish and administer pension schemes and funds.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The Social Security Act of 1973 made further provisions "for occupational pensions, established the Occupational Pensions Board and the contributory reserve pension scheme under a Reserve Pension Board for not recognised pensionable employment."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Scottish_nationalism">Scottish nationalism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Scottish nationalism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Scottish_National_Party" title="Scottish National Party">Scottish nationalism</a> grew as a political force, while the <a href="/wiki/Decimal_Day" title="Decimal Day">decimalisation of British coinage</a>, begun under the previous Labour government, was completed eight months after Heath came to power. The <a href="/wiki/Central_Policy_Review_Staff" title="Central Policy Review Staff">Central Policy Review Staff</a> was established by Heath in February 1971,<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> while the <a href="/wiki/Local_Government_Act_1972" title="Local Government Act 1972">Local Government Act 1972</a> changed the boundaries of the counties of England and Wales and created Metropolitan Counties around the major cities (e.g. Merseyside around Liverpool): this caused significant public anger. Heath did not divide England into regions, choosing instead to await the report of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_on_the_Constitution_(United_Kingdom)" title="Royal Commission on the Constitution (United Kingdom)">Crowther Commission</a> on the constitution; the 10 <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_England" title="Regions of England">Government Office Regions</a> were eventually set up by the Major government in 1994.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_policy">Economic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Economic policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Chancellor of the Exchequer <a href="/wiki/Iain_Macleod" title="Iain Macleod">Iain Macleod</a> died and was replaced on 20 July 1970 by <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Barber" title="Anthony Barber">Anthony Barber</a>. Heath's planned economic policy changes (including a significant shift from direct to indirect taxation) remained largely unimplemented: the Selsdon policy document was more or less abandoned as unemployment increased considerably by 1972. By January that year, the number of unemployed reached a million, the highest level for more than two decades. Opposed to unemployment on moral grounds, Heath encouraged a famous "U-Turn" in economic policy that precipitated what became known as the "<a href="/wiki/Barber_Boom" class="mw-redirect" title="Barber Boom">Barber Boom</a>". This was a two-range process involving the budgets of <a href="/wiki/1972_United_Kingdom_budget" title="1972 United Kingdom budget">1972</a> and 1973, the former of which pumped £2.5&#160;billion into the economy in increased pensions and benefits and tax reductions. By early 1974, as a result of this Keynesian economic strategy, unemployment had fallen to under 550,000. The economic boom did not last, and the Heath government implemented various cuts that led to the abandonment of policy goals such as a planned expansion of nursery education.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_41-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Trade_unions">Trade unions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Trade unions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Much of the government's attention, as well as the media and public opinion, focused on deteriorating labour relations, as the government sought to weaken the economic power of the trade unions, which had grown steadily since 1945. The <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Relations_Act_1971" title="Industrial Relations Act 1971">Industrial Relations Act 1971</a> set up a special court under the judge <a href="/wiki/John_Donaldson,_Baron_Donaldson_of_Lymington" title="John Donaldson, Baron Donaldson of Lymington">Lord Donaldson</a>. Its imprisonment of striking dockworkers was a public relations disaster and became an object lesson for the Thatcher government of the 1980s. Thatcher relied instead on confiscating the assets of unions that courts found to have violated anti-strike laws.
</p><p>The trade unions responded with a full-scale counterattack on a government hobbled by inflation and high unemployment. Especially damaging to the government's credibility were the two miners' strikes of 1972 and 1974, the latter of which resulted in much of the country's industry working a <a href="/wiki/Three-Day_Week" title="Three-Day Week">Three-Day Week</a> in an attempt to conserve energy. The <a href="/wiki/National_Union_of_Mineworkers_(Great_Britain)" title="National Union of Mineworkers (Great Britain)">National Union of Mineworkers</a> won its case but the energy shortages and the resulting breakdown of domestic consensus contributed to the eventual downfall of his government.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Unemployment">Unemployment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Unemployment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>There was a steep rise in unemployment for the first two years of the Heath ministry, but it was then reversed. Labour in 1964 had inherited an unemployment count of around 400,000 but saw unemployment peak at 631,000 in early 1967. At election time in June 1970, the unemployment numbers were still high at 582,000. Heath and the Conservatives were pledged to "full employment" but within a year it became clear that they were losing that battle, as the official unemployment count crept towards 1,000,000 and some newspapers suggested that it was even higher. In January 1972 it was officially confirmed that unemployment had risen above 1,000,000 – a level not seen for more than 30 years.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Various other reports around this time suggested that unemployment was higher still, with <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i> newspaper claiming that "nearly 3,000,000" people were jobless by March of that year.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Enlargement_of_the_European_Union#First_enlargement" title="Enlargement of the European Union">Enlargement of the European Union §&#160;First enlargement</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:204px;max-width:204px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a
