decoding="async" width="200" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Besuch_von_Premierminister_von_Gro%C3%9Fbritanniend_Eward_Heath_in_Holtenau_%28Kiel_53.857%29.jpg/300px-Besuch_von_Premierminister_von_Gro%C3%9Fbritanniend_Eward_Heath_in_Holtenau_%28Kiel_53.857%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Besuch_von_Premierminister_von_Gro%C3%9Fbritanniend_Eward_Heath_in_Holtenau_%28Kiel_53.857%29.jpg/400px-Besuch_von_Premierminister_von_Gro%C3%9Fbritanniend_Eward_Heath_in_Holtenau_%28Kiel_53.857%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="682" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">Heath with German <a href="/wiki/Christian_Democratic_Union_of_Germany" title="Christian Democratic Union of Germany">CDU</a> politician <a href="/wiki/Uwe_Barschel" title="Uwe Barschel">Uwe Barschel</a> in 1972</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Upon entering office in June 1970, Heath immediately set about trying to reverse Wilson's policy of ending Britain's military presence <a href="/wiki/East_of_Suez" title="East of Suez">East of Suez</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Heath took the United Kingdom into Europe on 1 January 1973, following passage in Parliament of the <a href="/wiki/European_Communities_Act_1972_(UK)" title="European Communities Act 1972 (UK)">European Communities Act 1972</a> in October.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> He publicly supported the <a href="/wiki/Operation_Linebacker_II" title="Operation Linebacker II">massive U.S. bombing</a> of <a href="/wiki/Hanoi" title="Hanoi">Hanoi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Haiphong" title="Haiphong">Haiphong</a> in December 1972.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His government quickly recognised the military regime of <a href="/wiki/Augusto_Pinochet" title="Augusto Pinochet">Augusto Pinochet</a> in <a href="/wiki/Military_dictatorship_of_Chile_(1973%E2%80%931990)" title="Military dictatorship of Chile (1973–1990)">Chile</a> and maintained good relations with it, despite the illegal nature of the Pinochet regime's <a href="/wiki/1973_Chilean_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1973 Chilean coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 1973 he placed a British arms embargo on all combatants in the Arab-Israeli <a href="/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War" title="Yom Kippur War">Yom Kippur War</a>, which mostly affected the Israelis by preventing them obtaining spares for their Centurion tanks. Heath refused to allow US intelligence gathering from <a href="/wiki/Akrotiri_and_Dhekelia" title="Akrotiri and Dhekelia">British bases in Cyprus</a>, resulting in a temporary halt in the US signals intelligence tap.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> He also refused permission for the US to use any British bases for resupply.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He favoured links with the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China_(1949%E2%80%931976)" title="History of the People&#39;s Republic of China (1949–1976)">People's Republic of China</a>, visiting <a href="/wiki/Mao_Zedong" title="Mao Zedong">Mao Zedong</a> in Beijing in 1974 and 1975 and remaining an honoured guest in China on frequent visits thereafter and forming a close relationship with Mao's successor <a href="/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping" title="Deng Xiaoping">Deng Xiaoping</a>. Heath realised that to become closer to Europe he needed to be further from the United States, so he downplayed the <a href="/wiki/Special_Relationship" title="Special Relationship">Special Relationship</a> that had long knitted the two nations together. The two nations differed on such major crises as Britain's EC membership, the <a href="/wiki/Nixonomics" title="Nixonomics">Nixon economic "shocks" of 1971</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War" title="Bangladesh Liberation War">Bangladesh Liberation War</a>, détente with Soviet Union, Kissinger's Year of Europe and the Middle East crisis of 1973.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Northern_Ireland">Northern Ireland</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Northern Ireland">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Heath governed during a bloody period in the history of the <a href="/wiki/The_Troubles" title="The Troubles">Northern Ireland Troubles</a>. On <a href="/wiki/Bloody_Sunday_(1972)" title="Bloody Sunday (1972)">Bloody Sunday in 1972</a>, 14 men and youths were shot dead by British soldiers during an anti-internment march in <a href="/wiki/Derry" title="Derry">Derry City</a>. In early 1971 Heath sent in a <a href="/wiki/Secret_Intelligence_Service" class="mw-redirect" title="Secret Intelligence Service">Secret Intelligence Service</a> officer, Frank Steele, to talk to the IRA and find out what common ground there was for negotiations. Steele had carried out secret talks with <a href="/wiki/Jomo_Kenyatta" title="Jomo Kenyatta">Jomo Kenyatta</a> ahead of the British withdrawal from <a href="/wiki/Kenya" title="Kenya">Kenya</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> In July 1972, Heath permitted his Secretary of State for Northern Ireland, William Whitelaw, to hold unofficial talks in London with an IRA delegation by <a href="/wiki/Se%C3%A1n_Mac_St%C3%ADof%C3%A1in" title="Seán Mac Stíofáin">Seán Mac Stíofáin</a>. In the aftermath of these unsuccessful talks, the Heath government pushed for a peaceful settlement with the political parties exclusively committed to non-violence.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 1973 <a href="/wiki/Sunningdale_Agreement" title="Sunningdale Agreement">Sunningdale Agreement</a>, which proposed a power-sharing deal, was strongly repudiated by many Unionists and the <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Unionist_Party" title="Ulster Unionist Party">Ulster Unionist Party</a> who withdrew its MPs at Westminster from the Conservative whip. The proposal was finally brought down by the Loyalist <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Workers%27_Council_strike" title="Ulster Workers&#39; Council strike">Ulster Workers' Council strike</a> in 1974, by which time Heath was no longer in office.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Heath was targeted by the IRA for introducing internment in Northern Ireland. In December 1974, the <a href="/wiki/Balcombe_Street_siege" title="Balcombe Street siege">Balcombe Street</a> <a href="/wiki/Active_service_unit" title="Active service unit">ASU</a> threw a bomb onto the first-floor balcony of his home in Wilton Street, <a href="/wiki/Belgravia" title="Belgravia">Belgravia</a> where it exploded. Heath had been conducting a Christmas carol concert at Broadstairs and arrived home 10 minutes after the bomb exploded. No one was injured in the attack, but a landscape painted by Winston Churchill – given to Heath as a present – was damaged.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2003, Heath gave evidence to the <a href="/wiki/Saville_Inquiry" class="mw-redirect" title="Saville Inquiry">Saville Inquiry</a> and stated that he had never sanctioned unlawful lethal force in Northern Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Fall_from_power">Fall from power</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Fall from power">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ford_A0655_NLGRF_photo_contact_sheet_(1974-09-09)(Gerald_Ford_Library)_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Ford_A0655_NLGRF_photo_contact_sheet_%281974-09-09%29%28Gerald_Ford_Library%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Ford_A0655_NLGRF_photo_contact_sheet_%281974-09-09%29%28Gerald_Ford_Library%29_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c4/Ford_A0655_NLGRF_photo_contact_sheet_%281974-09-09%29%28Gerald_Ford_Library%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="284" data-file-height="188" /></a><figcaption>Heath greeting US president <a href="/wiki/Gerald_Ford" title="Gerald Ford">Gerald Ford</a> in September 1974</figcaption></figure> 
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Second_Shadow_Cabinet_of_Edward_Heath" title="Second Shadow Cabinet of Edward Heath">Second Shadow Cabinet of Edward Heath</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1974_general_elections">1974 general elections</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: 1974 general elections">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Heath tried to bolster his government by calling a <a href="/wiki/February_1974_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="February 1974 United Kingdom general election">general election</a> for 28 February 1974, using the election slogan "Who governs Britain?". The result of the election was inconclusive with no party gaining an overall majority in the House of Commons; the Conservatives had the most votes but Labour had slightly more seats. Heath began negotiations with <a href="/wiki/Jeremy_Thorpe" title="Jeremy Thorpe">Jeremy Thorpe</a>, leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal Party</a> but, when these failed, he resigned as prime minister on 4 March 1974, and was replaced by Wilson's minority Labour government, eventually confirmed, though with a tiny majority, in a second election in October.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> To date, Heath is the last British Prime Minister to have both assumed and lost their term of office by means of a General Election. 
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rise_of_Thatcher">Rise of Thatcher</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Rise of Thatcher">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Heath came to be seen as a liability by many Conservative MPs, party activists and newspaper editors. His personality was considered cold and aloof, annoying even to his friends. <a href="/wiki/Alan_Watkins" title="Alan Watkins">Alan Watkins</a> observed in 1991 that his "brusqueness, his gaucherie, his lack of small or indeed any talk, his sheer bad manners" were among the factors costing him the support of Conservative backbenchers in the subsequent <a href="/wiki/1975_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="1975 Conservative Party leadership election">Conservative leadership election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He resolved to remain Conservative leader, even after losing the October 1974 general election, and at first it appeared that by calling on the loyalty of his front-bench colleagues he might prevail. In the weeks following the second election defeat, Heath came under tremendous pressure to concede a review of the rules and agreed to establish a commission to propose changes and to seek re-election. There was no clear challenger after <a href="/wiki/Enoch_Powell" title="Enoch Powell">Enoch Powell</a> had left the party and <a href="/wiki/Keith_Joseph" title="Keith Joseph">Keith Joseph</a> had ruled himself out after controversial statements implying that the working classes should be encouraged to use more birth control. Joseph's close friend and ally <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a>, who believed that an adherent to the philosophy of the <a href="/wiki/Centre_for_Policy_Studies" title="Centre for Policy Studies">Centre for Policy Studies</a> should stand, joined the leadership contest in his place alongside the outsider <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Fraser_(British_politician)" title="Hugh Fraser (British politician)">Hugh Fraser</a>. Aided by <a href="/wiki/Airey_Neave" title="Airey Neave">Airey Neave</a>'s campaigning among backbench MPs — whose earlier approach to <a href="/wiki/William_Whitelaw,_1st_Viscount_Whitelaw" title="William Whitelaw, 1st Viscount Whitelaw">William Whitelaw</a> had been rebuffed, out of loyalty to Heath — she emerged as the only serious challenger.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The new rules permitted new candidates to enter the ballot in a second round of voting should the first be inconclusive, so Thatcher's challenge was considered by some to be that of a stalking horse. Neave deliberately understated Thatcher's support to attract wavering votes from MPs who were keen to see Heath replaced even though they did not necessarily want Thatcher to replace him.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 4 February 1975, Thatcher defeated Heath in the first ballot by 130 votes to 119, with Fraser coming in a distant third with 16 votes. This was not a big enough margin to give Thatcher the 15% majority necessary to win on the first ballot, but having finished in second place Heath immediately resigned and did not contest the next ballot. His favoured candidate, William Whitelaw, lost to Thatcher in the second vote one week later (Thatcher 146, Whitelaw 79, <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Howe" title="Geoffrey Howe">Howe</a> 19, <a href="/wiki/Jim_Prior" title="Jim Prior">Prior</a> 19, <a href="/wiki/John_Peyton,_Baron_Peyton_of_Yeovil" title="John Peyton, Baron Peyton of Yeovil">Peyton</a> 11).<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> The vote polarised along right-left lines, with in addition the region, experience and education of the MP having their effects. Heath and Whitelaw were stronger on the left, among Oxbridge and public school graduates, and in MPs from Northern England or Scotland.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thatcher had promised Heath a seat in the Shadow Cabinet and planned to offer him whatever post he wanted. His advisors agreed he should wait at least six months, so he declined. He never relented and his refusal was called "the incredible sulk".<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Thatcher visited Heath at his home shortly after her election as leader and had to stay for coffee with his PPS <a href="/wiki/Timothy_Kitson" title="Timothy Kitson">Timothy Kitson</a> so the waiting press would not realise how brief the visit had been. Heath claimed that he had simply declined her request for advice about how to handle the press, whilst Thatcher claimed that she offered him any Shadow Cabinet position he wanted and asked him to lead the Conservative campaign in the imminent <a href="/wiki/1975_United_Kingdom_European_Communities_membership_referendum" title="1975 United Kingdom European Communities membership referendum">EEC referendum</a>, only to be rudely rebuffed.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_career_.281975.E2.80.932001.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_career_(1975–2001)">Later career (1975–2001)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Later career (1975–2001)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Edward_Heath_appearing_on_%27After_Dark%27,_10_June_1989.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Edward_Heath_appearing_on_%27After_Dark%27%2C_10_June_1989.jpg/220px-Edward_Heath_appearing_on_%27After_Dark%27%2C_10_June_1989.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="156" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Edward_Heath_appearing_on_%27After_Dark%27%2C_10_June_1989.jpg/330px-Edward_Heath_appearing_on_%27After_Dark%27%2C_10_June_1989.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Edward_Heath_appearing_on_%27After_Dark%27%2C_10_June_1989.jpg/440px-Edward_Heath_appearing_on_%27After_Dark%27%2C_10_June_1989.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1351" data-file-height="960" /></a><figcaption>Appearing on television discussion programme <i><a href="/wiki/After_Dark_(TV_programme)" title="After Dark (TV programme)">After Dark</a></i> in 1989</figcaption></figure>
<p>For many years, Heath persisted in criticism of the party's new ideological direction. At the time of his defeat, he was still popular with rank-and-file Conservative members and was warmly applauded at the 1975 Conservative Party Conference. He played a leading role in the 1975 referendum campaign in which the UK voted to remain part of the EEC, and he remained active on the international stage, serving on the <a href="/wiki/Willy_Brandt" title="Willy Brandt">Brandt</a> Commission investigation into developmental issues, particularly on <a href="/wiki/Global_North_and_Global_South" title="Global North and Global South">North–South</a> projects (<a href="/wiki/Brandt_Report" title="Brandt Report">Brandt Report</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His relations with Thatcher remained poor, and in 1979–80, he turned down her offers of the positions of <a href="/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_to_the_United_States" title="List of ambassadors to the United States">Ambassador to the United States</a> and <a href="/wiki/Secretary_General_of_NATO" title="Secretary General of NATO">Secretary General of NATO</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> He continued as a central figure on the left of the party and, at the 1981 Conservative Party conference, openly criticised the government's economic policy of <a href="/wiki/Monetarism" title="Monetarism">monetarism</a>, which had seen inflation rise from 13% in 1979 to 18% in 1980 then fall to 4% by 1983,<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> but had seen unemployment double from around 1.5&#160;million to a postwar high of 3.3&#160;million during that time.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1990, he flew to <a href="/wiki/Baghdad" title="Baghdad">Baghdad</a> to attempt to negotiate the release of aircraft passengers on <a href="/wiki/British_Airways_Flight_149" title="British Airways Flight 149">British Airways Flight 149</a> and other British nationals taken hostage when <a href="/wiki/Saddam_Hussein" title="Saddam Hussein">Saddam Hussein</a> invaded <a href="/wiki/Kuwait" title="Kuwait">Kuwait</a>. After the events of <a href="/wiki/Black_Wednesday" title="Black Wednesday">Black Wednesday</a> in 1992, he stated in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> that government should build a fund of reserves to counter currency speculators.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 1987, he was nominated in the <a href="/wiki/1987_University_of_Oxford_Chancellor_election" title="1987 University of Oxford Chancellor election">election for the Chancellorship of the University of Oxford</a> but lost to <a href="/wiki/Roy_Jenkins" title="Roy Jenkins">Roy Jenkins</a> as a result of splitting the Conservative vote with <a href="/wiki/Robert_Blake,_Baron_Blake" title="Robert Blake, Baron Blake">Lord Blake</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sir_Edward_Heath_1995.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Sir_Edward_Heath_1995.jpg/170px-Sir_Edward_Heath_1995.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="247" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Sir_Edward_Heath_1995.jpg/255px-Sir_Edward_Heath_1995.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Sir_Edward_Heath_1995.jpg/340px-Sir_Edward_Heath_1995.jpg 2x" data-file-width="706" data-file-height="1024" /></a><figcaption>Heath in 1995</figcaption></figure>
<p>Heath continued to serve as a backbench MP for the London constituency of <a href="/wiki/Old_Bexley_and_Sidcup_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Old Bexley and Sidcup (UK Parliament constituency)">Old Bexley and Sidcup</a> and was, from 1992, the longest-serving MP ("<a href="/wiki/Father_of_the_House_(United_Kingdom)" title="Father of the House (United Kingdom)">Father of the House</a>") and the oldest British MP. As Father of the House, he oversaw the election of two <a href="/wiki/Speaker_(politics)" title="Speaker (politics)">Speakers</a> of the Commons, <a href="/wiki/Betty_Boothroyd" title="Betty Boothroyd">Betty Boothroyd</a> and <a href="/wiki/Michael_Martin,_Baron_Martin_of_Springburn" title="Michael Martin, Baron Martin of Springburn">Michael Martin</a>. Heath was created a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Knight of the Garter</a> on 23 April 1992.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> He retired from Parliament at the <a href="/wiki/2001_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="2001 United Kingdom general election">2001 general election</a>. Heath and <a href="/wiki/Tony_Benn" title="Tony Benn">Tony Benn</a> were the last two serving MPs to have been elected during the reign of <a href="/wiki/George_VI" title="George VI">George&#160;VI</a>, with Heath serving continuously since 1950.
</p><p>Heath maintained business links with several companies including a Saudi think tank, two investment funds and a Chinese freight operator, mainly as an adviser on China or a member of the governing board.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Chris_Patten" title="Chris Patten">Chris Patten</a>, the last <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_Hong_Kong" title="Governor of Hong Kong">Governor of Hong Kong</a>, his commercial interests in China could have been one of the reasons why he denounced the democratic reforms introduced in the run-up to the <a href="/wiki/Handover_of_Hong_Kong" title="Handover of Hong Kong">handover of Hong Kong</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Parliament broke with precedent by commissioning a bust of Heath while he was still alive.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Commentators<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (August 2021)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> have noted how the statue of <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a> appears to overshadow Heath's bust. The 1993 bronze work, by <a href="/wiki/Martin_Jennings" title="Martin Jennings">Martin Jennings</a>, was moved to the <a href="/wiki/Members%27_Lobby" title="Members&#39; Lobby">Members' Lobby</a> in 2002. On 29 April 2002, in his eighty-sixth year, he made a public appearance at Downing Street alongside the then–prime minister <a href="/wiki/Tony_Blair" title="Tony Blair">Tony Blair</a> and the three other surviving former prime ministers at the time (<a href="/wiki/James_Callaghan" title="James Callaghan">James Callaghan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Major" title="John Major">John Major</a>), as well as relatives of deceased prime ministers, for a dinner which was part of the <a href="/wiki/Golden_Jubilee_of_Elizabeth_II" title="Golden Jubilee of Elizabeth II">Golden Jubilee of Elizabeth II</a>. This was to be one of his last public appearances, as the following year saw a decline in his health.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Private_residence">Private residence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Private residence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Edward_Heath_Allan_Warren.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Edward_Heath_Allan_Warren.jpg/170px-Edward_Heath_Allan_Warren.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="294" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Edward_Heath_Allan_Warren.jpg/255px-Edward_Heath_Allan_Warren.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Edward_Heath_Allan_Warren.jpg/340px-Edward_Heath_Allan_Warren.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3647" data-file-height="6314" /></a><figcaption>Heath in his Salisbury home, 1987</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sir_Edward_Heath_-_Arundells_59_Cathedral_Close_Salisbury_SP1_2EN.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Sir_Edward_Heath_-_Arundells_59_Cathedral_Close_Salisbury_SP1_2EN.jpg/220px-Sir_Edward_Heath_-_Arundells_59_Cathedral_Close_Salisbury_SP1_2EN.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Sir_Edward_Heath_-_Arundells_59_Cathedral_Close_Salisbury_SP1_2EN.jpg/330px-Sir_Edward_Heath_-_Arundells_59_Cathedral_Close_Salisbury_SP1_2EN.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Sir_Edward_Heath_-_Arundells_59_Cathedral_Close_Salisbury_SP1_2EN.jpg/440px-Sir_Edward_Heath_-_Arundells_59_Cathedral_Close_Salisbury_SP1_2EN.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1500" /></a><figcaption>Blue plaque located on the boundary wall of Arundells</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the 1960s, Heath had lived in the <a href="/wiki/Albany_(London)" title="Albany (London)">Albany</a>, off Piccadilly; at the unexpected end of his premiership, the French couple living there refused his demand that they move out so that he could have his flat back ("So much for European Unity!" Heath later wrote in his memoirs). For four months, Heath took the flat of Conservative MP <a href="/wiki/Timothy_Kitson" title="Timothy Kitson">Timothy Kitson</a>; Kitson declined his offer to pay rent but later recalled an occasion when his own watch broke, and Heath in response invited him to take one of a large collection that he had been given on his travels. In July 1974, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Grosvenor,_5th_Duke_of_Westminster" title="Robert Grosvenor, 5th Duke of Westminster">the Duke of Westminster</a>, a major London landowner and ardent Europhile, allowed Heath to rent a property in Wilton Street, Belgravia, for an annual rent of £1,250 (just under £10,000 at 2014 prices), a tenth of the market value. The house had three storeys and a basement flat for Heath's housekeeper, and he continued to use it as his London home until old age prevented him from climbing the stairs.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 1985, Heath acquired a Wiltshire home, <a href="/wiki/Arundells" title="Arundells">Arundells</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_close" title="Cathedral close">Cathedral close</a> at <a href="/wiki/Salisbury" title="Salisbury">Salisbury</a>, where he resided until his death twenty years later. In January 2006, it was announced that Heath had placed his house and contents, valued at £5&#160;million in his will, in a charitable foundation, the Sir Edward Heath Charitable Foundation, to conserve the house as a museum to his career.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The house is open to the public for guided tours from March to October; displayed therein is a large collection of personal effects as well as Heath's personal library, photo collections, and paintings by Winston Churchill.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his will, Heath, who had no descendants, left only two legacies: £20,000 to his brother's widow, and £2,500 to his housekeeper.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Yachting">Yachting</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Yachting">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Heath was a keen yachtsman. He bought his first yacht <i><a href="/wiki/Morning_Cloud" title="Morning Cloud">Morning Cloud</a></i> in 1969 and won the <a href="/wiki/Sydney_to_Hobart_Yacht_Race" title="Sydney to Hobart Yacht Race">Sydney to Hobart Yacht Race</a> that year. He captained Britain's winning team for the <a href="/wiki/Admiral%27s_Cup" title="Admiral&#39;s Cup">Admiral's Cup</a> in 1971<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> – while prime minister – and also captained the team in the <a href="/wiki/1979_Fastnet_race" class="mw-redirect" title="1979 Fastnet race">1979 Fastnet race</a>. He was a member of the Broadstairs Sailing Club, where he learnt to sail on a <a href="/wiki/Snipe_(dinghy)" title="Snipe (dinghy)">Snipe</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Fireball_(dinghy)" title="Fireball (dinghy)">Fireball</a> before moving on to success in larger boats.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Classical_music">Classical music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Classical music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Heath maintained an interest in classical music as a pianist, organist and orchestral conductor, famously installing a <a href="/wiki/Steinway_%26_Sons" title="Steinway &amp; Sons">Steinway</a> grand in <a href="/wiki/10_Downing_Street" title="10 Downing Street">10&#160;Downing Street</a> – bought with his £450 <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne_Prize" title="Charlemagne Prize">Charlemagne Prize</a> money, awarded for his unsuccessful efforts to bring Britain into the EEC in 1963, and chosen on the advice of his friend, the pianist <a href="/wiki/Moura_Lympany" title="Moura Lympany">Moura Lympany</a> – and conducting Christmas carol concerts in Broadstairs every year from his teens until old age.
</p><p>Heath conducted the <a href="/wiki/London_Symphony_Orchestra" title="London Symphony Orchestra">London Symphony Orchestra</a>, notably at a gala concert at the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Festival_Hall" title="Royal Festival Hall">Royal Festival Hall</a> in November 1971, at which he conducted <a href="/wiki/Edward_Elgar" title="Edward Elgar">Sir Edward Elgar</a>'s overture <i><a href="/wiki/Cockaigne_(In_London_Town)" title="Cockaigne (In London Town)">Cockaigne (In London Town)</a></i>. He also conducted the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Liverpool_Philharmonic" title="Royal Liverpool Philharmonic">Royal Liverpool Philharmonic</a> and the <a href="/wiki/English_Chamber_Orchestra" title="English Chamber Orchestra">English Chamber Orchestra</a>, as well as orchestras in Germany and the United States. During his premiership, Heath invited musician friends, such as <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Stern" title="Isaac Stern">Isaac Stern</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yehudi_Menuhin" title="Yehudi Menuhin">Yehudi Menuhin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Clifford_Curzon" title="Clifford Curzon">Clifford Curzon</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Amadeus_Quartet" title="Amadeus Quartet">Amadeus Quartet</a>, to perform either at <a href="/wiki/Chequers" title="Chequers">Chequers</a> or 10 Downing Street. Heath was the founding President of the European Community Youth Orchestra (in 1976), now the <a href="/wiki/European_Union_Youth_Orchestra" title="European Union Youth Orchestra">European Union Youth Orchestra</a>.
</p><p>In 1988, Heath recorded <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_van_Beethoven" title="Ludwig van Beethoven">Beethoven's</a> <a href="/wiki/Triple_Concerto_(Beethoven)" title="Triple Concerto (Beethoven)">Triple Concerto, Op.&#160;56</a> (with members of the Trio Zingara as soloists) and <a href="/wiki/Luigi_Boccherini" title="Luigi Boccherini">Boccherini's</a> Cello Concerto in G major, G480.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Football">Football</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Football">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Heath was a supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Lancashire" title="Lancashire">Lancashire</a> football club <a href="/wiki/Burnley_F.C." title="Burnley F.C.">Burnley</a>, and just after the end of his term as prime minister in 1974 he opened the £450,000 <a href="/wiki/Bob_Lord_(football_chairman)" title="Bob Lord (football chairman)">Bob Lord</a> Stand at the club's <a href="/wiki/Turf_Moor" title="Turf Moor">Turf Moor</a> stadium.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Author">Author</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Author">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Boeken,_politici,_Bestanddeelnr_929-1293.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Boeken%2C_politici%2C_Bestanddeelnr_929-1293.jpg/220px-Boeken%2C_politici%2C_Bestanddeelnr_929-1293.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="224" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Boeken%2C_politici%2C_Bestanddeelnr_929-1293.jpg/330px-Boeken%2C_politici%2C_Bestanddeelnr_929-1293.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Boeken%2C_politici%2C_Bestanddeelnr_929-1293.jpg/440px-Boeken%2C_politici%2C_Bestanddeelnr_929-1293.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2525" data-file-height="2566" /></a><figcaption>Heath <a href="/wiki/Book_signing" title="Book signing">book-signing</a> in 1977</figcaption></figure>
<p>Heath wrote several books in the second half of the 1970s: <i>Sailing</i>, <i>Music</i>, and <i>Travels</i>. He also compiled a collection of carols called <i>The Joy of Christmas</i>, published in 1978 by Oxford University Press, which contained the music and lyrics to a wide variety of Christmas carols, each accompanied by a reproduction of a piece of religious art and a short introduction by Heath.
</p><p>Heath's autobiography, <i>The Course of My Life</i>, appeared in 1998. According to his obituary in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Daily_Telegraph" title="The Daily Telegraph">The Daily Telegraph</a></i>, this "had involved dozens of researchers and writers (some of whom he never paid) over many years".<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id=".22Grocer_Heath.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="&quot;Grocer_Heath&quot;">"Grocer Heath"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: &quot;Grocer Heath&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1964, despite substantial opposition from many Conservative MPs and independent grocers and shopkeepers, Heath led a successful fight to abolish <a href="/wiki/Resale_price_maintenance" title="Resale price maintenance">resale price maintenance</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-RPM_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RPM-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><i><a href="/wiki/Private_Eye" title="Private Eye">Private Eye</a></i>, a satirical current affairs magazine, thereupon persistently ridiculed him as "Grocer Heath".<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> The magazine also parodied him as the managing director of a struggling small company, "Heathco".<sup id="cite_ref-Gresham_Ramsden_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gresham_Ramsden-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-The_Guardian_Hastings_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Guardian_Hastings-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sexuality">Sexuality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Sexuality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Heath never married. He had been expected to marry childhood friend Kay Raven, who was reportedly tired of waiting and married an RAF officer whom she met on holiday in 1950. In a four-sentence paragraph of his memoirs, Heath claimed that he had been too busy establishing a career after the war and had "perhaps&#160;... taken too much for granted". In a 1998 TV interview with Michael Cockerell, Heath said that he had kept her photograph in his flat for many years afterwards.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His interest in music kept him on friendly terms with female musicians, including pianist Moura Lympany. When Heath was prime minister she was approached by the Conservative MP <a href="/wiki/Tufton_Beamish,_Baron_Chelwood" title="Tufton Beamish, Baron Chelwood">Tufton Beamish</a>, who said: "Moura, Ted must get married. Will you marry him?" She said she would have done but was in love with someone else.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> She later said the most intimate thing Heath had done was to put his arm around her shoulder.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Bernard_Levin" title="Bernard Levin">Bernard Levin</a> wrote at the time in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Observer" title="The Observer">The Observer</a></i> that the UK had to wait until the emergence of the <a href="/wiki/Permissive_society" title="Permissive society">permissive society</a> for a prime minister who was a virgin.<sup id="cite_ref-guardian.co.uk_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardian.co.uk-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> In later life, according to his official biographer <a href="/wiki/Philip_Ziegler" title="Philip Ziegler">Philip Ziegler</a>, at dinner parties Heath was "apt to relapse into morose silence or completely ignore the woman next to him and talk across her to the nearest man";<sup id="cite_ref-guardian.co.uk_31-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardian.co.uk-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> others at the time claimed Heath was just not talkative at parties.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There were many innuendos in <i>Private Eye</i> about it and chants insinuating he was homosexual could be heard outside Downing Street during protests by trade unionists against his Industrial Relations Bill.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Campbell, who published a biography of Heath in 1993,<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> devoted four pages to a discussion of the evidence concerning Heath's sexuality. While acknowledging that Heath was often assumed by the public to be gay, not least because it is "nowadays&#160;... whispered of any bachelor", he found "no positive evidence" that this was so "except for the faintest unsubstantiated rumour" (the footnote refers to a mention of a "disturbing incident" at the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a> in a 1972 biography by <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Roth" title="Andrew Roth">Andrew Roth</a>). Campbell ultimately concluded that the most significant aspect of Heath's sexuality was his complete <a href="/wiki/Psychological_repression" class="mw-redirect" title="Psychological repression">repression</a> of it.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Brian_Coleman" title="Brian Coleman">Brian Coleman</a>, the Conservative Party London Assembly member for <a href="/wiki/Barnet_and_Camden_(London_Assembly_constituency)" title="Barnet and Camden (London Assembly constituency)">Barnet and Camden</a>, claimed in 2007 that Heath, to protect his career, had stopped <a href="/wiki/Cottaging" title="Cottaging">cottaging</a> in the 1950s. Coleman said it was "common knowledge" among Conservatives that Heath had been given a stern warning by police when he underwent background checks for the post of <a href="/wiki/Privy_councillor" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy councillor">privy councillor</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Heath's biographer Philip Ziegler wrote in 2010 that Coleman was able to provide "little or no information" to back up this statement, that no man had ever claimed to have had a sexual relationship with Heath, nor was any trace of homosexuality to be found in his papers, and that "those who knew him well" insist that he had no such inclination. He believes Heath to have been <a href="/wiki/Asexuality" title="Asexuality">asexual</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> although he does mention a letter from one "Freddy", who seems hurt that "Teddie" had spurned his advances (Chapter 2 of his book).
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Robert_Armstrong,_Baron_Armstrong_of_Ilminster" title="Robert Armstrong, Baron Armstrong of Ilminster">Lord Armstrong of Ilminster</a>, who was Heath's friend and former <a href="/wiki/Principal_Private_Secretary_to_the_Prime_Minister" class="mw-redirect" title="Principal Private Secretary to the Prime Minister">private secretary</a>, stated his belief that Heath was asexual, saying that he "never detected a whiff of sexuality in relation to men, women or children."<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Another friend and confidant, Sara Morrison, former vice-chairman of the Conservative Party, said Heath had "effectively" told her "that he was sexless".<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Charles_Moore_(journalist)" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles Moore (journalist)">Charles Moore</a>, in his authorised biography of Margaret Thatcher, said that <a href="/wiki/Bill_Deedes" title="Bill Deedes">Bill Deedes</a> believed that Thatcher "seem[ed] convinced" Heath was gay, whilst Moore believed it is "possible" that Thatcher's reference, in interview in 1974, to Heath not having a family, was a deliberate hint that he was gay, in order to discredit him.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Thatcher certainly seems to have disliked Heath. "When I look at him and he looks at me," she once remarked, according to Ziegler (Chapter 4), "it doesn't feel like a man looking at a woman, more like a woman looking at another woman."
</p><p>When he moved to Arundells in 1985, Heath hired Derek Frost, life partner of <a href="/wiki/Jeremy_Norman" title="Jeremy Norman">Jeremy Norman</a>, to modernise and redecorate the house in Salisbury. He became friends with the couple, though never close. When they asked Heath why he had not supported homosexual law reform (he was either absent from the debates in the 1960s or voted against <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Gore,_8th_Earl_of_Arran" title="Arthur Gore, 8th Earl of Arran">Lord Arran</a>'s first Bill in May 1965), he replied that he had always been in favour but that "the rank and file of the party would never have stood for it." Norman's view is that Heath was "a deeply closeted gay man" who "decided early in life to sublimate his sexuality to his political ambitions."<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> In later life, Heath voted for the lowering of the age of same-sex consent to eighteen and then sixteen.
</p><p>Similarly, Michael McManus, who was Heath's private secretary in the 1990s and helped with his memoirs, writes in his book on gay conservative politicians that he "was left in no doubt whatsoever that Heath was a gay man who had sacrificed his personal life to his political career, exercising iron self-control and living a celibate existence as he climbed the 'greasy pole' of preferment."<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Allegations_of_child_sexual_abuse">Allegations of child sexual abuse</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Allegations of child sexual abuse">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Elm_Guest_House_hoax" title="Elm Guest House hoax">Elm Guest House hoax</a></div>
<p>In April 2015, a rape claim against Heath was investigated by the <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Police" title="Metropolitan Police">Metropolitan Police</a> but was dropped.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> In August 2015, several police forces were investigating allegations of <a href="/wiki/Child_sexual_abuse" title="Child sexual abuse">child sexual abuse</a> by Heath.<sup id="cite_ref-SKY1530217_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SKY1530217-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Hampshire, Jersey, Kent, Wiltshire, Gloucestershire and Thames Valley constabularies and London's Metropolitan Police investigated such claims.<sup id="cite_ref-bbc-33770021_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc-33770021-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> It was reported that a man had claimed that at the age of 12 years he had been raped by Heath in a <a href="/wiki/Mayfair" title="Mayfair">Mayfair</a> flat in 1961, after he had run away from home.<sup id="cite_ref-Independent_Anderson_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Independent_Anderson-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Allegations about Heath were investigated as part of <a href="/wiki/Operation_Midland" title="Operation Midland">Operation Midland</a>, the Metropolitan Police inquiry into claims of historic child abuse and related <a href="/wiki/Homicide" title="Homicide">homicides</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> A witness called "Nick" was introduced to the police by the former <a href="/wiki/Exaro" title="Exaro">Exaro</a> website, who had asked him about alleged child sexual abuse by prominent figures at the <a href="/wiki/Dolphin_Square" title="Dolphin Square">Dolphin Square</a> apartment complex in <a href="/wiki/Pimlico" title="Pimlico">Pimlico</a>, London; Heath was reported to be one of the figures.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> In 2018 "Nick", whose real name is <a href="/wiki/Carl_Beech_(Operation_Midland)" class="mw-redirect" title="Carl Beech (Operation Midland)">Carl Beech</a>, was arrested and charged over child pornography offences<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> and in January 2019 he pleaded guilty.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Beech, who had fabricated allegations against Heath and other prominent politicians and civil servants, was sentenced in July 2019 to eighteen years in prison.<sup id="cite_ref-Beech_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Beech-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Also in August 2015, <a href="/wiki/Sky_News" title="Sky News">Sky News</a> reported that Jersey police were investigating allegations against Heath as part of <a href="/wiki/Operation_Whistle" title="Operation Whistle">Operation Whistle</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-SKY1530217_150-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SKY1530217-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> and a similar investigation, Operation Conifer, was launched by <a href="/wiki/Wiltshire_Police" title="Wiltshire Police">Wiltshire Police</a> at the same time.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> The Sir Edward Heath Charitable Foundation, which operates the museum at Arundells, his home in Salisbury, said it welcomed the investigation.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> In November 2016, criminologist <a href="/wiki/Richard_Hoskins" title="Richard Hoskins">Richard Hoskins</a> said that the evidence used against Heath in Operation Conifer, including discredited allegations of <a href="/wiki/Satanic_ritual_abuse" class="mw-redirect" title="Satanic ritual abuse">satanic ritual abuse</a>, was "preposterous", "fantastical" and gained through the "controversial" practice of <a href="/wiki/Recovered-memory_therapy" title="Recovered-memory therapy">recovered-memory therapy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-guardian-20161127_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardian-20161127-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Operation Conifer was closed in March 2017, having cost a reported £1.5&#160;million over two years, as no corroborating evidence had been found in any of the 42 allegations by 40 individuals (including three different names used by one person).<sup id="cite_ref-PE1455_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PE1455-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 2017, it was announced that the <a href="/wiki/Independent_Inquiry_into_Child_Sexual_Abuse" title="Independent Inquiry into Child Sexual Abuse">Independent Inquiry into Child Sexual Abuse</a> would review the police investigation into Heath.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Police said that if Heath were still alive they would have interviewed him <a href="/wiki/Right_to_silence_in_England_and_Wales" title="Right to silence in England and Wales">under caution</a> in relation to seven out of the 42 allegations,<sup id="cite_ref-PE1455_163-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PE1455-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> but nothing should be inferred about his guilt or innocence.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> In his summary report, Chief Constable <a href="/wiki/Mike_Veale" title="Mike Veale">Mike Veale</a> confirmed that "no further corroborative evidence was found" to support the satanic abuse claims.<sup id="cite_ref-PE1455_163-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PE1455-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Illness_and_death">Illness and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Illness and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Edward_Heath_monument.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Edward_Heath_monument.jpg/170px-Edward_Heath_monument.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Edward_Heath_monument.jpg/255px-Edward_Heath_monument.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Edward_Heath_monument.jpg/340px-Edward_Heath_monument.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1944" data-file-height="2592" /></a><figcaption>Heath's monument in <a href="/wiki/Salisbury_Cathedral" title="Salisbury Cathedral">Salisbury Cathedral</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In August 2003, at the age of 87, Heath suffered a <a href="/wiki/Pulmonary_embolism" title="Pulmonary embolism">pulmonary embolism</a> while on holiday in <a href="/wiki/Salzburg" title="Salzburg">Salzburg</a>, Austria. He never fully recovered, and owing to his declining health and mobility made very few public appearances in the last two years of his life, his last one was at the unveiling of a set of gates at <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral" title="St Paul&#39;s Cathedral">St Paul's Cathedral</a> dedicated to Churchill on 30 November 2004.
</p><p>In his final public statement, Heath paid tribute to <a href="/wiki/James_Callaghan" title="James Callaghan">James Callaghan</a>, who died on 26 March 2005, saying "James Callaghan was a major fixture in the political life of this country during his long and varied career. When in opposition he never hesitated to put firmly his party's case. When in office he took a smoother approach towards his supporters and opponents alike. Although he left the House of Commons in 1987 he continued to follow political life and it was always a pleasure to meet with him. We have lost a major figure from our political landscape."<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Just under four months later, Sir Edward Heath died at his home from <a href="/wiki/Pneumonia" title="Pneumonia">pneumonia</a> at 7.30<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>pm on 17 July 2005, at the age of 89.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> He was cremated on 25 July 2005 at a funeral service attended by 1,500 people. On the day after his death, the <a href="/wiki/BBC_Parliament" title="BBC Parliament">BBC Parliament</a> channel showed the BBC results coverage of the 1970 election. A memorial service was held for Heath in <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a> on 8 November 2005, which was attended by 2,000 people. Three days later his ashes were interred in <a href="/wiki/Salisbury_Cathedral" title="Salisbury Cathedral">Salisbury Cathedral</a>. In a tribute to him, the then-prime minister Tony Blair stated "He was a man of great integrity and beliefs he held firmly from which he never wavered".<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Heath_ministry">Heath ministry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Heath ministry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="excerpt-block"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066933788">.mw-parser-output .excerpt-hat .mw-editsection-like{font-style:normal}</style><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable dablink excerpt-hat selfref">This section is an excerpt from <a href="/wiki/Heath_ministry#June_1970_–_March_1974" title="Heath ministry">Heath ministry § June 1970 – March 1974</a>.<span class="mw-editsection-like plainlinks"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Heath_ministry&amp;action=edit">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div><div class="excerpt">
<p>The cabinet appointed in June 1970 comprised the following:<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister</a>: <b>Edward Heath</b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord High Chancellor of Great Britain</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_of_St_Marylebone" title="Quintin Hogg, Baron Hailsham of St Marylebone">The Lord Hailsham of St Marylebone</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/William_Whitelaw,_1st_Viscount_Whitelaw" title="William Whitelaw, 1st Viscount Whitelaw">William Whitelaw</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Lords" title="Leader of the House of Lords">Leader of the House of Lords</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/George_Jellicoe,_2nd_Earl_Jellicoe" title="George Jellicoe, 2nd Earl Jellicoe">The Earl Jellicoe</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Iain_Macleod" title="Iain Macleod">Iain Macleod</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Foreign_Secretary" title="Foreign Secretary">Foreign Secretary</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home" title="Alec Douglas-Home">Sir Alec Douglas-Home</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Home_Secretary" title="Home Secretary">Home Secretary</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Reginald_Maudling" title="Reginald Maudling">Reginald Maudling</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Agriculture,_Fisheries_and_Food" title="Minister of Agriculture, Fisheries and Food">Minister of Agriculture, Fisheries and Food</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/James_Prior" class="mw-redirect" title="James Prior">James Prior</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Defence" title="Secretary of State for Defence">Secretary of State for Defence</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Peter_Carington,_6th_Baron_Carrington" title="Peter Carington, 6th Baron Carrington">The Lord Carrington</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Education" title="Secretary of State for Education">Secretary of State for Education and Science</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Employment" title="Secretary of State for Employment">Secretary of State for Employment</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Leonard_Robert_Carr" class="mw-redirect" title="Leonard Robert Carr">Robert Carr</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Housing_and_Local_Government" title="Ministry of Housing and Local Government">Minister of Housing and Local Government</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Peter_Walker,_Baron_Walker_of_Worcester" title="Peter Walker, Baron Walker of Worcester">Peter Walker</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Health_and_Social_Services" title="Secretary of State for Health and Social Services">Secretary of State for Health and Social Services</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Keith_Joseph" title="Keith Joseph">Sir Keith Joseph</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Duchy_of_Lancaster" title="Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster">Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Anthony_Barber" title="Anthony Barber">Anthony Barber</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Scotland" title="Secretary of State for Scotland">Secretary of State for Scotland</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Gordon_Campbell,_Baron_Campbell_of_Croy" title="Gordon Campbell, Baron Campbell of Croy">Gordon Campbell</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Technology" title="Ministry of Technology">Minister for Technology</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Rippon" title="Geoffrey Rippon">Geoffrey Rippon</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Trade" title="President of the Board of Trade">President of the Board of Trade</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Michael_Noble,_Baron_Glenkinglas" title="Michael Noble, Baron Glenkinglas">Michael Noble</a></b></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Wales" title="Secretary of State for Wales">Secretary of State for Wales</a>: <b><a href="/wiki/Peter_Thomas,_Baron_Thomas_of_Gwydir" title="Peter Thomas, Baron Thomas of Gwydir">Peter Thomas</a></b></li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Changes">Changes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Module:Excerpt&amp;action=edit&amp;section=T-1" title="Edit section: Changes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>July 1970 – <a href="/wiki/Iain_Macleod" title="Iain Macleod">Iain Macleod</a> dies, and is succeeded as Chancellor by <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Barber" title="Anthony Barber">Anthony Barber</a>. <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Rippon" title="Geoffrey Rippon">Geoffrey Rippon</a> succeeds Barber as Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster. <a href="/wiki/John_Davies_(British_businessman)" title="John Davies (British businessman)">John Davies</a> succeeds Rippon as Secretary for Technology.</li>
<li>October 1970 – The Ministry of Technology and the Board of Trade are merged to become the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Trade_and_Industry_(United_Kingdom)" title="Department of Trade and Industry (United Kingdom)">Department of Trade and Industry</a>. <a href="/wiki/John_Davies_(British_businessman)" title="John Davies (British businessman)">John Davies</a> becomes <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Business_and_Trade" title="Secretary of State for Business and Trade">Secretary of State for Trade and Industry</a>. <a href="/wiki/Michael_Noble,_Baron_Glenkinglas" title="Michael Noble, Baron Glenkinglas">Michael Noble</a> leaves the cabinet. The Ministry of Housing and Local Government is succeeded by the new department of the Environment which was headed by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Walker,_Baron_Walker_of_Worcester" title="Peter Walker, Baron Walker of Worcester">Peter Walker</a>.</li>
<li>March 1972 – <a href="/wiki/Robert_Carr" title="Robert Carr">Robert Carr</a> succeeds William Whitelaw as Lord President and Leader of the House of Commons. <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Macmillan" title="Maurice Macmillan">Maurice Macmillan</a> succeeds Carr as Secretary for Employment. Whitelaw becomes Secretary of State for Northern Ireland.</li>
<li>July 1972 – <a href="/wiki/Robert_Carr" title="Robert Carr">Robert Carr</a> succeeds <a href="/wiki/Reginald_Maudling" title="Reginald Maudling">Reginald Maudling</a> as Home Secretary. <a href="/wiki/Jim_Prior" title="Jim Prior">Jim Prior</a> succeeds <a href="/wiki/Robert_Carr" title="Robert Carr">Robert Carr</a> as Lord President and Leader of the House of Commons. <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Godber" title="Joseph Godber">Joseph Godber</a> succeeds Prior as Secretary for Agriculture.</li>
<li>November 1972 – <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Rippon" title="Geoffrey Rippon">Geoffrey Rippon</a> succeeds <a href="/wiki/Peter_Walker,_Baron_Walker_of_Worcester" title="Peter Walker, Baron Walker of Worcester">Peter Walker</a> as Secretary for the Environment. <a href="/wiki/John_Davies_(British_businessman)" title="John Davies (British businessman)">John Davies</a> succeeds Rippon as Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster. Peter Walker succeeds Davies as Secretary for Trade and Industry. <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Howe" title="Geoffrey Howe">Geoffrey Howe</a> becomes Minister for Trade and Consumer Affairs with a seat in the cabinet.</li>
<li>June 1973 – <a href="/wiki/David_Hennessy,_3rd_Baron_Windlesham" title="David Hennessy, 3rd Baron Windlesham">The Lord Windlesham</a> succeeds Lord Jellicoe as Lord Privy Seal and Leader of the House of Lords.</li>
<li>December 1973 – <a href="/wiki/William_Whitelaw,_1st_Viscount_Whitelaw" title="William Whitelaw, 1st Viscount Whitelaw">William Whitelaw</a> succeeds <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Macmillan" title="Maurice Macmillan">Maurice Macmillan</a> as Secretary for Employment. <a href="/wiki/Francis_Pym" title="Francis Pym">Francis Pym</a> succeeds Whitelaw as Secretary for Northern Ireland. Macmillan becomes Paymaster-General.</li>
<li>January 1974 – <a href="/wiki/Ian_Gilmour,_Baron_Gilmour_of_Craigmillar" title="Ian Gilmour, Baron Gilmour of Craigmillar">Ian Gilmour</a> succeeds Lord Carrington as Secretary for Defence; Lord Carrington becomes <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Energy_and_Climate_Change" title="Secretary of State for Energy and Climate Change">Secretary of State for Energy</a>.</li></ul></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output <p>Edward Heath received several accolades and honours.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Coat_of_arms">Coat of arms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Coat of arms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<table class="wikitable mw-collapsible" style="max-width: 100%">
<caption class="nowrap">Coat of arms of Edward Heath
</caption>
<tbody><tr>
<td style="width: 220px;"><figure class="mw-halign-center" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Arms_of_Edward_Heath.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Arms_of_Edward_Heath.svg/150px-Arms_of_Edward_Heath.svg.png" decoding="async" width="150" height="181" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Arms_of_Edward_Heath.svg/225px-Arms_of_Edward_Heath.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Arms_of_Edward_Heath.svg/300px-Arms_of_Edward_Heath.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="617" /></a><figcaption></figcaption></figure>
</td>
<td style="min-width:175px;">
<dl><dt>Crest</dt>
<dd>Out of a Naval Coronet Or, a Swan close proper resting its foot on a closed Cup Or.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup></dd>
<dt>Escutcheon</dt>
<dd>Per bend Purpure and Vert, over all a Bend grady Or, issuant in sinister chief a Cloud, irradiated proper, and in dexter base a Portcullis chained Or.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup></dd>
<dt>Supporters</dt>
<dd>On the dexter Sea-Lion Or charged with a voided Escutcheon Vert and on the sinister a Horse Argent gorged with a Ducal Coronet Vert.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup></dd>
<dt>Motto</dt>
<dd><b>Plus Fait Douceur Que Violence</b><br /><i>(lit. Sweetness does more than violence)</i></dd>
<dt>Symbolism</dt>
<dd>The arms symbolise various parts of Heath's life. The bend grady represents the zig-zag pattern of the Royal Artillery tie, as well as Broadstairs, his birthplace. The green colour is for heath, while the purple is for heather, which refers to his surname. The cloud symbolises 'Morning Cloud', his yacht. The irradiations issuing from it symbolise entering the European communities. The portcullis is the symbol of Parliament and represents his career as a politician.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> The swan and the gold cup in the crest represent two livery companies: the <a href="/wiki/Worshipful_Company_of_Musicians" title="Worshipful Company of Musicians">Worshipful Company of Musicians</a> (of which he was an honorary freeman) and the <a href="/wiki/Goldsmiths%27_Company" class="mw-redirect" title="Goldsmiths&#39; Company">Goldsmiths' Company</a> (of which he was a liveryman), respectively. Heath's captaincy of yachting teams is symbolised by the naval crown in the crest. The sea-lion and white horse supporters are both taken from the Kent county coat of arms, honouring the county that Heath was born in as well as the location of his parliamentary constituency. The voided escutcheon on the sea-lion's neck also appears in the arms of <a href="/wiki/Balliol_College" class="mw-redirect" title="Balliol College">Balliol College</a>, Heath's alma mater. The horse is gorged with a ducal coronet, which refers to the crown in the arms of the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Salisbury" title="Diocese of Salisbury">Diocese of Salisbury</a>. This symbolises the location of Heath's home in Salisbury. Heath's motto was taken from a motto that appeared on his clavichord.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Honorary_degrees">Honorary degrees</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_Heath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Honorary degrees">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
